All planar two-loop amplitudes in maximally supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory
Anne Spiering, Matthias Wilhelm, Chi Zhang (张驰)

Abstract
We calculate the general planar dual-conformally invariant double-pentagon and pentabox integrals in four dimensions. Concretely, we derive one-fold integral representations for these elliptic integrals over polylogarithms of weight three. These integral representations allow us to determine the respective symbols using consistency conditions alone. Together with the previously calculated double-box integral, these integrals suffice to express all two-loop planar scattering amplitudes in maximally supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory in four dimensions – which we thus calculate for any number of particles and any helicity configuration!



HU-EP-24/18-RTG
|
BONN-TH-2024-09

I
Introduction

Since the solution of the one-loop scattering problem
in planar maximally supersymmetric Yang-Mills (
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM) theory
[
1
]
over 30 years ago, significant advances have been made both in our computational capabilities,
and in revealing hidden structures in general Quantum Field Theory amplitudes.
These achievements are partly due to the full mastery over the structure of one-loop amplitudes, exemplified by techniques such as unitarity methods and the on-shell formalism
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
; see e.g. refs.
[
6
,
7
]
for a review.

While impressive progress in computing
specific multi-loop amplitudes and Feynman integrals has been achieved, see e.g. ref.
[
8
]
, there remains a lack of knowledge about the general two-loop scattering problem, even in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory.
The main difficulty for such computations is the appearance of functions beyond multiple polylogarithms (MPLs)
[
9
]
.
The most prominent and well-studied example is the sunrise integral
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, which contains an elliptic curve and
integrates to elliptic multiple polylogarithms (eMPLs)
[
15
,
16
]
.
Planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory offers an excellent framework for studying scattering amplitudes due to the absence of ultraviolet divergences and full control of the infrared (IR) behaviour
[
17
,
18
]
.
Notably, the first elliptic Feynman integral in this theory was identified in a specific component of the two-loop amplitude: the ten-point double-box integral
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
.
Its result in terms of eMPLs was first given in ref.
[
23
]
and its singularity structure was studied therein by means of symbol techniques
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
.

In this letter, as a first step towards a comprehensive understanding of general two-loop amplitudes,
we present a one-fold integral representation and the symbol for
all two-loop amplitudes
in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory, regardless of the multiplicity and helicity configuration of the external particles.
This progress is based on the known reduction of two-loop amplitudes into double-box, pentabox and double-pentagon Feynman integrals in four dimensions
[
27
,
28
]
:

(1)

where the sums run over all planar momentum configurations and the coefficients can be obtained via unitarity methods.
The (twelve-point) double-box integral is the best understood of these integrals, in particular due to a first-order differential equation relating it to the one-loop hexagon integral in six dimensions
[
20
,
21
]
; cf. Sec.
II.1
. The latter was explicitly evaluated as a weight-three MPL
[
29
]
, thus allowing for a one-fold integral representation over polylogarithms for the double box.
This one-fold representation was the basis for determining its symbol in ref.
[
30
]
.
In this letter, we find similar differential equations and integral representations for the pentabox and double pentagon (Sec.
II.2
and
II.3
).
As part of the effort to fully understand planar two-loop scattering, we also study the singularity structure of these integrals, in particular generalising the simplicity found in the double-box symbol
[
30
]
to the case of the remaining two-loop integrals, see Sec.
III
.

II
Differential equations and integral representations

In this section, we describe the first-order differential equations satisfied by the pentabox and double-pentagon integrals, along with their resulting one-fold integral representations, which are similar to those of the double-box integral. To lay the groundwork, we begin by introducing our notations and reviewing the notable structures of the double-box integral, which will reappear in the pentabox and double-pentagon integrals, albeit in a slightly more intricate fashion.

II.1
Review: Double box

The most general double-box integral has six off-shell momenta
p_{i}^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,

i=1,...,6
, with
p_{i}^{2}\neq 0
.
For planar massless Feynman integrals, it has proven useful to transition to the dual space with coordinates
x_{i}^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
via
p_{i}=x_{i+1}-x_{i}
, cf. Fig.
1
,
as this manifests a dual conformal symmetry of the associated Feynman integrals
[
31
]
.
A conventional way to linearise this symmetry is to adopt the embedding-space formalism
[
32
,
33
]
, where generally a point
x^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is mapped to a projective light ray
X^{M}=[1:x^{2}:x^{\mu}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
(in light-cone coordinates), such that

(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}=-2X_{i}\cdot X_{j}=:(X_{i},X_{j})=:X_{ij}\,.

(2)

In these coordinates, the double-box integral reads

where

and

are the index sets for the dual points

.

on the projective null cone is similarly defined as in ref.

]
, see App.

for details.

(4)

with
\partial_{L}:={\textstyle\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset L}}X_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial{X_{ij}}}
, which relates the double box to the one-loop hexagon in six dimensions, a polylogarithm of weight three
[
29
]
.

The differential equation (
4
) can be recast into an integral relation.
In order to do so, we
introduce a multiplicatively independent basis of dual-conformally invariant (DCI) cross ratios,

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{X_{i,j-1}X_{i-1,j}}{X_{i,j}X_{i-1,j-1}}\bigg|~\text{non-adjacent }\{i,j\}\subset[n]\right\}\,,

(5)

with
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
In the double-box case with
n=6
, there are nine such variables which we denote as
u_{a}
,
a\in[9]
, with respect to the lexicographical order of
\{i,j\}
.
For future convenience, we also introduce Gram determinants in embedding space as

\mathcal{G}_{B}^{A}:=\det(X_{ab})_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
b\in B\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \mathcal{G}_{A}:=\mathcal{G}_{A}^{A}\>,\hskip 9.24994ptA,B\subset[n]\,.

(6)

They can be normalised by products of
X_{ij}
to make them DCI, and we usually normalise such that they become
polynomials
in the
u_{a}
’s;
for instance
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[6]}:=\mathcal{G}_{[6]}/(X_{14}X_{25}X_{36})^{2}
, where the hat indicates normalisation.

) via

is a polynomial of degree three in

indicates the appearance of an elliptic curve in the double box. Nevertheless, it is as-yet-unknown whether the integral (
7
) can be integrated to eMPLs.

Below we will see that the pentabox and double-pentagon integrals can similarly be expressed as (sums over) one-fold integrals of hexagons, with
u_{2}
generalising to the respective cross ratios
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
of the four points around the
middle

2
(a) and
3
(a).
To simplify the notation, we introduce
\widehat{f}(x):=\widehat{f}(u_{2(3)}\to x)
for a DCI function
\widehat{f}
where the corresponding cross ratio
u_{2}
(or
u_{3}
) is replaced by the integration variable
x
.

to reduce the heptagon to a sum of six-dimensional hexagons, with subscript

Relations (
9
) and (
10
) can be easily verified in the Mellin-space framework of refs.
[
20
,
21
]
, or via Feynman-parameter representations obtained by loop-by-loop integration
[
35
]
.
A subtle point to highlight is that in deriving eq. (
10
) we assumed all
X_{ij}
’s to be independent.
However, these
X_{ij}
’s are subject to a constraint,
\mathcal{G}_{[7]}=0
, in four-dimensional kinematics.
At the level of the 14 cross ratios of eq. (
5
) (with
n=7
), this becomes the constraint
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[7]}=0
, which reduces the number of independent kinematic degrees of freedom to 13 and implies

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[7]}(x)\propto(x-u_{2})(x-r_{+})(x-r_{-})\,,

(12)

where now
u_{2}:=X_{13}X_{47}/(X_{14}X_{37})
and the replacement
u_{2}\to x
is understood to occur before restricting to four dimensions.
The constraint
\mathcal{G}_{[7]}=0
does not commute with the differential operator in eq. (
10
) and thus we first solve this equation for generic
X_{ij}
’s, resulting in a one-fold integral representation over hexagon integrals (via eq. (
11
)) for the six-dimensional pentaboxes, and afterwards restrict to four-dimensional external kinematics.

Together with eq. (
9
), eqs. (
10
)–(
11
) lead to a one-fold integral representation over hexagon integrals:

, which states that massless planar Feynman integrals can only become singular at

. This apparent contradiction is resolved by the relation

(a).
This integral depends on 17 independent cross ratios in four-dimensional external kinematics.

The story here is analogous to the pentabox case: the double pentagon in four dimensions can be obtained from the six-dimensional scalar double pentagon with the middle propagator being doubled by acting with the operator

\hat{\mathcal{D}}:=-(N_{L},N_{R})-\!\!\!\!\sum_{\ell\in L,r\in R}\!\!\!\!(N_{L},X_{r})(N_{R},X_{\ell})\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{\ell r}}\,,

(17)

while the scalar double pentagon is related to the octagon in eight dimensions via
(1+\partial_{L})
, cf. Fig.
3
.

In the first term on the RHS, the integrals (
14
) reappear, with
[\hat{\imath}]:=[8]\setminus\{i\}
indicating the relevant pentabox index sets, together with the prefactor
\mathsf{LS}_{\text{pb}[\hat{\imath}]}^{(\pm)}
corresponding to the sum/difference of the double-pentagon residues on the associated pentabox cut. In the second term,
[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]:=[8]\setminus\{\ell,r\}
indicates the index set for the hexagon integrals and
y_{\ell r}^{2}(x):=-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(x)
is cubic in
x
in all cases, i.e. there appear 16 elliptic curves in this expression, corresponding to the 16 double-box subtopologies of the double pentagon. Finally,
\mathsf{LS}_{\text{kb}}

is a linear combination of residues on the kissing-box cut and the
C_{\ell r}
’s
are rational functions of the kinematics. See App.
A
for more explicit expressions.

III
Symbol

In this section, we study the symbols and coproducts of the pentabox and double-pentagon integral.
For a general
n
-fold MPL or eMPL
I_{n}
, the symbol can be defined through the total differential
dI_{n}=\sum_{\alpha}I_{n-1}^{(\alpha)}df_{\alpha}
, where
\{f_{\alpha}\}
is a set of one-fold integrals:

\mathcal{S}(I_{n})=\textstyle\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{S}(I_{n-1}^{(\alpha)})\otimes f_{\alpha}\,.

(19)

Although some information is lost in this mapping, it uncovers key algebraic properties of the original integral, such as functional identities and its singularity structure
[
39
,
24
,
40
,
41
]
.
The elliptic symbol bootstrap
[
30
]
and the algorithm developed in ref.
[
42
]
have in some cases been successful at computing symbols of elliptic Feynman integrals when their explicit forms in terms of eMPLs are unknown.
However, leveraging the structure of the double-box symbol, the one-fold integral representations from the previous section, and further known properties of (elliptic) symbols, we will see that the pentabox and double-pentagon symbols are highly constrained, essentially allowing us to write down the answer directly!

The symbol of the double-box integral
[
30
]
can be organised into a compact
\Delta_{2,2}
coproduct, defined in a similar way to eq. (
19
).
2
2

2

Roughly speaking, the coproduct can be thought of as a partial uplift of the symbol to the full function; for instance, eq. (
19
) can be understand as
\Delta_{n-1,1}(I_{n})=\textstyle\sum_{\alpha}I_{n-1}^{(\alpha)}\otimes f_{\alpha}
in terms of
\Delta_{n-1,1}
coproducts. See refs.
[
43
,
44
,
41
]
for further details.
Specifically,

A technical but crucial detail is that the integration kernel in eq. (
23
) is a combination of two pure integration kernels defined on the torus
\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}\tau)
associated to the elliptic curve, with modular parameter
\tau=\omega_{2}/\omega_{1}
.
Specifically,

\displaystyle\frac{dx~y(c)}{y(x)(x{-}c)}=dw(x)\Bigl(\partial_{z}\tilde{\Omega}(z,w(c))\big|_{z=w(x)}\!-\partial_{z}\tilde{\Omega}(z,w(c))\big|_{z=0}\Bigr)

(24)

with the torus coordinate
w({\bullet}):=\omega^{-1}_{1}\int^{\bullet}dx/y(x)
,
and
\tilde{\Omega}(z,w):=\log\frac{\theta_{1}(z-w|\tau)}{\theta_{1}(z+w|\tau)}
, where
\theta_{1}
denotes the odd Jacobi theta function.
In the notation of ref.
[
48
]
, the first integration kernel on the RHS of eq. (
24
) corresponds to
dx\Psi_{-1}(c,x)
and the latter is related to the double-box integration kernel
dx/y(x)
.

Finally, let us mention that in the double-pentagon case, the only new ingredient is an integral of the form (

) with

. Its
\Delta_{2,2}
-coproduct and symbol can be easily obtained from the pentabox expressions by taking

; see App.

for the explicit results.

IV
Discussion and Outlook

In this letter, we have calculated the general pentabox and double-pentagon integrals, which together with the previously calculated double-box integral give all two-loop amplitudes in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory.
Concretely, we derived one-fold integral representations for the pentabox and double-pentagon integrals by generalising the differential-equation approach of refs.
[
20
,
21
]
.
From these integral representations, we were essentially able to write down the corresponding symbols as the most naive expressions that satisfy basic consistency requirements! These expressions hold true because of significant cancellations in four-dimensional kinematics.

It remains unknown whether these integrals can be evaluated as eMPLs, but the one-fold integral representation offers a promising alternative.
This representation provides an excellent starting point for computing the symbol and thus for studying the singularity structure of Feynman integrals,
as demonstrated in refs.
[
30
,
42
]
and here.
For numerical evaluations, it has been shown
[
51
]
that one-fold integral representations also offer significant improvements
for some degenerate cases of the integrals discussed here. However, achieving a complete numerical evaluation for the most general case requires a detailed study of the integration contour as well as the analytic continuation of the one-loop hexagon into various kinematic regions, which we leave for future work.

The entire amplitude (
1
) also includes integrals where some massive legs become massless or soft, potentially introducing IR divergences.
The one-fold integral representation, together with the DCI regulator
[
52
]
, has already provided a convenient solution for the double box, as shown in refs.
[
26
,
53
]
.
The pentabox and double pentagon do not introduce additional complexity, as they only become divergent when the massive legs on the box side become massless.

The two-loop results presented here pave the way for numerous new computations in planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory and even in QCD.
Beyond these
immediate applications, just as the BCF and BCFW relations
[
3
,
4
]
provided deep insights from one-loop amplitudes,
we are more keen to ask: what new understandings about the structure of perturbative Quantum Field Theory can be gained from these two-loop results?

Appendix A
Integral representations

In this appendix, we provide further details on the one-fold integral representations given in Sec.
II
.

The integral measure on the projective light cone is defined as

\int D^{d}X:=\int\frac{2\delta(X^{2})\>d^{d+2}X}{\pi^{d/2}\operatorname{Vol}\operatorname{GL}(1,\mathbb{R})^{+}}\,,

(32)

such that

\Gamma(d)\int\frac{D^{d}X}{(X,Y)^{d}}=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{(-Y\cdot Y)^{d/2}}\,.

(33)

With this integral measure,
the Feynman-parameter representations for the integrals in the main text are as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\hbox to14.14pt{\vbox to10.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.0684pt\lower-5.03691pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.35 L 0 3.35}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty]^{7}}\frac{2\,[d^{3}\vec{\alpha}_{L}]d^{4}\vec{\alpha}_{R}}{(-Y_{L}\cdot Y_{L})(-Y_{[8]}\cdot Y_{[8]})^{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\left(3\frac{(N_{L},Y_{R})(N_{R},Y_{L})}{-Y_{[8]}\cdot Y_{[8]}}-(N_{L},N_{R})\right)\,,

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty]^{7}}\frac{2[d^{3}\vec{\alpha}_{L}]d^{4}\vec{\alpha}_{R}}{(-Y_{L}\cdot Y_{L})(-Y_{[8]}\cdot Y_{[8]})^{3}}\,,

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[\hbox to14.04pt{\vbox to14.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.02007pt\lower-7.02007pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\infty]^{7}}\frac{6\,[d^{7}\vec{\alpha}_{[8]}]}{(-Y_{[8]}\cdot Y_{[8]})^{4}}\,,

(40)

where we have introduced
Y_{I}:=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}X_{i}
and
d^{\lvert I\rvert}\vec{\alpha}_{I}:=\prod_{i\in I}d\alpha_{i}

for the index set
I=L,R
or
[n]
. Additionally, we define
[d^{|I|-1}\vec{\alpha}_{I}]:=d^{|I|}\vec{\alpha}_{I}\delta(\alpha_{i}-1)
with arbitrary
i\in I
.

With the above Feynman-parameter representations, one can easily verify the differential equations (
4
) and (
10
). Alternatively, one can introduce an ancillary parameter
t
in
-Y_{[n]}\cdot Y_{[n]}
, such that

-Y_{[n]}^{2}(t):=-tY_{L}^{2}+(Y_{L},Y_{R})-Y_{R}^{2}\,.

(41)

Correspondingly,
t
-derivatives of these
t
-deformed two-loop integrals yield
t
-deformed one-loop Feynman integrals. The resulting one-fold integrals are related to those in the main text through the variable substitution
t=x/u_{2(3)}
. This approach avoids the argument about kinematic constraints and is more convenient for numerical purposes.

For a Gram determinant
\mathcal{G}_{I}
with the ordered index set
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
, we define its normalised version as

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{I}:=\frac{\mathcal{G}_{I}}{\prod_{a=1}^{n}(X_{i_{a}},X_{i_{a+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}})}\,,

(42)

where
i_{n+a}:=i_{a}
. We also encountered Gram determinants of the form
\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}]}^{[\hat{\jmath}]}
in the main text; their normalisations are the same as those of
\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[n]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}]}}
.

The sum/difference of the two leading singularities of the pentabox in eq. (
13
) are

\displaystyle\mathsf{LS}_{\text{pb}}^{(\pm)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2X_{13}}\Biggl(\frac{(N,Q_{L}^{+})}{(X_{2},Q_{L}^{+})\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{L}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{123Q_{L}^{+}}}}\pm(+\to-)\Biggr)\,,

(43)

where
Q_{L}^{\pm}
are two null vectors orthogonal to all
X_{\ell}
for
\ell\in L
. Moreover,
for
\{i,j\}\in R
, they satisfy

\frac{(Q_{L}^{\pm},X_{i})}{(Q_{L}^{\pm},X_{j})}=\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\{4567i\}}^{\{4567j\}}\pm\langle 4567ij\rangle\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{L}}}{\mathcal{G}_{\{4567j\}}}\,,

(44)

where
\langle ijklmn\rangle:=\epsilon_{MNPQRS}X_{i}^{M}X_{j}^{N}X_{k}^{P}X_{l}^{Q}X_{m}^{R}X_{n}^{S}
, and

\langle ijklmn\rangle^{2}=-\mathcal{G}_{\{ijklmn\}}
.

In eq. (
18
), the prefactor
\mathsf{LS}_{\text{pb}[\hat{\imath}]}^{(\pm)}
is given for
i\in L
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{LS}_{\text{pb}[\hat{\imath}]}^{(\pm)}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{57}X_{68}}{2}\Biggl(\frac{(N_{L},Q_{R}^{+})(N_{R},Q_{R}^{+})\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{L}^{L_{i}^{+}}}{\mathcal{G}_{L^{+}}\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{R}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{L_{i}^{+}}}}\pm(+\to-)\!\Biggr)\,,

(45)

where the normalisation of
\mathcal{G}_{L}^{L_{i}^{+}}
is the same as
\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{L}\mathcal{G}_{L_{i}^{+}}}
, and the index sets are
L^{\pm}:=L\cup\{Q_{R}^{\pm}\}
and
L_{i}^{\pm}=L^{\pm}\setminus\{i\}
. The result for
i\in R
can be easily obtained by the reflection
i\to 9-i
.
For the kissing-box configuration, the prefactor is given by

\mathsf{LS}_{\text{kb}}=\frac{1}{4}\!\!\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in(\pm,\pm)}\!\!\!\!\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\frac{(N_{L},Q_{L}^{\sigma_{1}})(N_{R},Q_{R}^{\sigma_{2}})}{\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{L}\mathcal{G}_{R}}(Q_{L}^{\sigma_{1}},Q_{R}^{\sigma_{2}})}\,.

(46)

Moreover, our convention for
y_{\ell r}(0)
is

y_{\ell r}(0)=\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\}}^{\{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\}}}{N_{\ell r}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with}\hskip 9.24994ptN_{\ell r}=\prod_{i=1}^{3}X_{\ell_{i},r_{i}}\,,

(47)

where
\{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\}=L\setminus\{\ell\}
and likewise for
\{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\}
.
To compute
C_{ij}
, we introduce two quadratic polynomials in
x
,

\displaystyle P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle:=(x-u_{3})^{-2}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[8]}(x)\,,

(48)

\displaystyle Q_{2}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell\in L,r\in R}\frac{(-1)^{\ell+r}(N_{L},X_{r})(N_{R},X_{\ell})}{(N_{L},N_{R})\prod_{i=1}^{4}X_{i,i+4}}N_{\ell r}\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{r}]}^{[\hat{\ell}]}(x)}{x-u_{3}}

and their
x
-derivatives
P^{\prime}_{2}(x)
,
P_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x)
, etc.
Then,

\displaystyle C_{\ell r}

\displaystyle=\frac{k(N_{L},N_{R})}{X_{14}X_{26}X_{37}X_{58}}\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(x-u_{3})\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{r}]}^{[\hat{\ell}]}(x)}{2\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[8]}(x)}\,,

(49)

where

k=\frac{P_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x)\bigl(P_{2}^{\prime}(x)-Q_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x)\bigr)}{\bigl(P_{2}^{\prime}(x)\bigr)^{2}-2P_{2}(x)P_{2}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\Bigg|_{x\to u_{3}}\,.

(50)

Appendix B
Symbol building blocks

In this appendix, we collect the (known) symbols for the one-loop box and double box, which are building blocks for the
pentabox and double-pentagon symbols.

{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 -3.94 L -10.08 -7.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\big]^{\text{\tiny(4d)}}_{\{ijkl\}}

(51)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Li}_{2}(z)-\operatorname{Li}_{2}(\bar{z})+\tfrac{1}{2}\log(z\bar{z})\log\frac{1-z}{1-\bar{z}}\,,

where

\displaystyle z\bar{z}=\frac{X_{ij}X_{kl}}{X_{ik}X_{jl}},\hskip 9.24994pt(1-z)(1-\bar{z})=\frac{X_{jk}X_{il}}{X_{ik}X_{jl}}\,,

(52)

with
z-\bar{z}=\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\{ijkl\}}}
.

Now, let us describe the symbol of
S_{ij}
appearing in the double-box
\Delta_{2,2}
-coproduct, cf. eq. (
20
).
For
\{i,j\}\subset L
(or
\{i,j\}\subset R
), with
\{i,j\}
cyclically ordered in
L
(or
R
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(S_{ij})=

\displaystyle\log R_{ij}(u_{2})\otimes 2\pi i\,w(u_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{\tau}S_{ij}\otimes 2\pi i\tau

(53)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=i,j\\
\sigma=\pm\end{subarray}}(\delta_{li}+\sigma\delta_{lj})\phi(p^{\sigma}_{l})\otimes 2\pi i\,w(p_{l}^{\sigma})\,.

If instead
i
and
j
take one value from each set,
e.g.
i=\ell\in L
and
j=r\in R
,
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(S_{\ell r})=\,\log R_{\ell r}(u_{2})\otimes 2\pi i\,w(u_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{\tau}S_{\ell r}\otimes 2\pi i\tau

(54)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a=\ell,r\\
\sigma=\pm\end{subarray}}\phi_{\ell r}(p_{a}^{\sigma})\otimes 2\pi i\,w(p_{a}^{\sigma})-(-1)^{\ell+r}\phi_{\ell r}(0)\otimes 2\pi i\,w(0)\,

with

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell r}(c)=\int^{u_{2}}_{\beta}\!\!\!\frac{dx~\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(c)}}{(x-c)\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(x)}}\,.

(55)

For
p_{i}^{\pm}
and their corresponding values
y(p_{i}^{\pm})
on the elliptic curve
y^{2}=-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[6]}(x)
,
we follow the convention used in ref.
[
30
]
:

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=u_{2}\Biggl(1-\frac{\left((-1)^{k+j}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}]}^{{}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{k}]}}\pm\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{k}]}}\right)X_{ij}X_{ik}}{2X_{12}X_{23}X_{31}X_{45}X_{56}X_{46}}\Biggr)\,,

\displaystyle y(p_{i}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i+j}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\imath}]}^{[\hat{\jmath}]}(x\to p_{i}^{\pm})\Big/\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}]}}\,,

(56)

where
\{i,j,k\}
is one of three cyclic permutations of either
\{1,2,3\}
or
\{4,5,6\}
, depending on the value of
i
.
As for
\phi_{\ell r}(p_{a}^{\sigma})
in eq. (
54
), we also need to determine
\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(p_{a}^{\sigma})}
. The convention we adopted is such that

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell r}(p_{\ell}^{+})\pm\phi_{\ell r}(p_{\ell}^{-})=\pm(-1)^{\ell+r}\log\frac{\mathcal{G}^{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}_{[\hat{\ell}\,\widehat{\ell\pm 1}]}-\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\,\widehat{\ell\pm 1}]}}}{\mathcal{G}^{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}_{[\hat{\ell}\,\widehat{\ell\pm 1}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\,\widehat{\ell\pm 1}]}}}\,,

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell r}(p_{r}^{+})\pm\phi_{ij}(p_{r}^{-})=\mp(-1)^{\ell+r}\log\frac{\mathcal{G}^{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}_{[\hat{r}\,\widehat{r\pm 1}]}-\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{r}\,\widehat{r\pm 1}]}}}{\mathcal{G}^{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}_{[\hat{r}\,\widehat{r\pm 1}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{r}\,\widehat{r\pm 1}]}}}\,.

(57)

Here, the indices
\ell\pm 1
should be understood as the cyclic successor/predecessor of
\ell
in the set
L
,
and likewise for
r
.

Note that the last entries
w(p_{l}^{\sigma})
are not independent; however, the integrability condition for the symbol of the double box is satisfied regardless of this fact.

Appendix C
Polylogarithmic symbols

In this appendix, we present the symbols associated to
the integrals
T_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(r_{\pm})
defined in the coproduct of the pentabox integrals (
27
) for
k\in R
, which are polylogarithmic. The results are actually independent of the particular values of
r_{\pm}
; thus, we will use
T_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)
with an arbitrary
c
below to simplify the notation.

Case (i)
:
\{i,j\}=R\backslash\{k\}
.
This case has no additional square roots arising from the four-mass box associated to indices
[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}\hat{k}]
,
and one can find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\big(T_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)\big)=

\displaystyle~\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(u_{2})\otimes\Phi_{k}(u_{2})

(58)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt-2\log\frac{4(c-u_{2})}{r^{[\hat{k}]}_{+}-r^{[\hat{k}]}_{-}}\otimes\Phi_{k}(\infty)\,,

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=\!\int^{c}_{r^{[\hat{k}]}_{\pm}}\!\frac{dx~y_{k}(a)}{(x-a)y_{k}(x)}=\!\!\int^{a}_{r^{[\hat{k}]}_{\pm}}\!\!\frac{dx~y_{k}(c)}{(x-c)y_{k}(x)}\,,

(59)

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(\infty)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)

(60)

and
y_{k}(x)
is quadratic in
x
, with roots in four-dimensional kinematics given by

r^{[\hat{k}]}_{\pm}=u_{2}\left(1+\frac{-\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{k}\hat{\imath}]}^{[\hat{k}\hat{\jmath}]}\pm\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{k}\hat{\imath}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{k}\hat{\jmath}]}}}{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{k}\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}]}X_{ij}}\right)\,.

(61)

Case (ii)
:
i\in R\backslash\{k\}
and
j\in L
.
This case has no additional square roots as well, and one can find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\big(T_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)\big)=\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(u_{2})\otimes\Phi_{k}(u_{2})+\phi(c)\otimes\log R^{[\hat{k}]}_{ij}(c)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt-\sum_{\sigma=\pm}(\delta_{i,k+1}+\sigma\delta_{i,k-1})~\phi(p_{kj}^{\sigma})\otimes\Phi_{k}(p_{kj}^{\sigma})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt-(-1)^{j}\log\frac{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}\hat{k}]}^{[\hat{1}\hat{2}\hat{3}]}-\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}\hat{k}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{1}\hat{2}\hat{3}]}}}{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}\hat{k}]}^{[\hat{1}\hat{2}\hat{3}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{\imath}\hat{\jmath}\hat{k}]}\mathcal{G}_{[\hat{1}\hat{2}\hat{3}]}}}\otimes\Phi_{k}(\infty)\,,

(62)

Similar to the case of the double box, we need to determine
y_{k}(p_{kj}^{\sigma})
to compute
\Phi_{k}(p_{kj}^{\sigma})
, and the convention we adopted is such that

\Phi_{k}(p_{kj}^{+})\pm\Phi_{k}(p_{kj}^{-})=\log R^{[\hat{k}]}_{k\pm 1,j}(c)\,,

(63)

where
k\pm 1
should again be understood as the cyclic successor/predecessor of
k
in the set
L
.

Case (iii)
:
\{i,j\}\subset L
.
This case involves an additional square root arising from the associated four-mass box, and we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\big(T^{[\hat{k}]}_{ij}(c)\big)=\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(u_{2})\otimes\Phi_{k}(u_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=i,j\\
\sigma=\pm\end{subarray}}\phi_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(p_{kl}^{\sigma})\otimes\Phi_{k}(p_{lk}^{\sigma})+\phi_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)\otimes\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(c)\,.

(64)

Note that
\phi_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(p_{kl}^{\sigma})
are not new objects; they can be computed by, e.g.,
\phi_{ij}^{[\hat{k}]}(p_{ki}^{\sigma})=\phi_{kj}^{[\hat{\imath}]}(p_{ki}^{\sigma})
, which we have already discussed in the case of the double box in App.
B
.

(65)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{i,j\}\subset[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}\widehat{\mathcal{I}}[\hbox to10.87pt{\vbox to10.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.28044pt\lower-5.43521pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{{}}{}

There are two cases: (i)
\{i,j\}
is a subset of
L
or
R
, and (ii)
i\in L
and
j\in R
. The case (i) is trivial; one can simply obtain the result by taking
r_{\pm}=0
in eq. (
III
). Note that, the last term in eq. (
III
) vanishes due to
\phi(0)=0
and then the integrability condition holds as we argued at the end of Sec.
III
. The case (ii) is less trivial; the last two terms in eq. (
29
) become divergent as
r_{\pm}\to 0
, but these divergences cancel each other. As a result, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{\pm}\to 0}-\Omega_{\ell r}^{\pm}(0)+(-1)^{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}\log R_{ij}^{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(r_{\pm})

\displaystyle=4\log\frac{\theta_{1}(w_{\ell r}(0))}{\eta(\tau_{\ell r})}+2\log\frac{a_{\ell r}}{4}-\tfrac{1}{3}\log\Delta_{\ell r}+\cdots\,,

(66)

where
i^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
are the ordinal numbers of
i
and
j
in the set
[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]
,

a_{\ell r}
is the leading coefficient of
-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(x)
in
x
,

\Delta_{\ell r}=g_{2}^{3}-27g_{3}^{2}
is the discriminant obtained from the standard Weierstrass form
Y^{2}=4X^{3}-g_{2}X-g_{3}
of the elliptic curve

y^{2}_{\ell r}=-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(x)
,
\eta
is the Dedekind eta function and its argument
\tau_{\ell r}
should be understood as the modular parameter of the elliptic curve
y^{2}_{\ell r}=-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{[\hat{\ell}\hat{r}]}(x)
. There are also some terms which cancel out in the sum in eq. (
14
); we omit these terms as indicated by the ellipsis.
